```json
    "oldString": "the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range",
    "newString": "the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range",
    "originalFile": "Learned sparsity predictors for SwiGLU LLMs predominantly use binary cross-entropy (BCE), training each neuron independently, yet inference allocates budgets globally via top-$k$ ranking.\nWe train lightweight predictors ($<$1\\% of base parameters) with forward KL divergence, coupling all mask decisions through the output distribution.\nOn LLaMA-3.1-8B at 30\\% sparsity, the KL predictor reduces perplexity by 14.5\\% over TEAL at matched sparsity, with directional commonsense gains ($n{=}3$) but knowledge-intensive regression (8B MMLU $-$5.2pp, CIs overlap; 14B GSM8K $-$19.9pp, $n{=}3$); post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) becomes redundant.\nOutput-distribution optimization yields masks that diverge 46--68\\% from magnitude-based targets; a target-swap ablation suggests these discovered patterns carry most of the quality information: BCE trained on KL-derived targets approaches KL-level PPL (PPL gap $\\leq$0.65pp; 3-seed, 30\\%).\nPer-layer controls show the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL) at 30\\%, though the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch; a $K{=}4$ control suggests task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and Qwen-2.5-14B confirms PPL generalization with scale-dependent downstream effects.\nThis is primarily an empirical study; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension, outperforming BCE/MSE across five losses and three architectures (7--14B) with 14.5\% PPL reduction at 30\%; gains are task-type dependent (commonsense up, knowledge down; C4 control confirms structural to KL), suggesting KL for commonsense/PPL, TEAL for knowledge tasks.
5	The signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs coupling breadth (2.08 PPL), though confounded (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}); downstream differences are directional ($n{=}3$). Code released upon acceptance.
6
```

### Tool result

_source line 11479 · 2026-05-24T03:58:29.606Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FijqFe3ZEEwyrnHrU1TkE3`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	How the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.
4	Learned sparsity predictors~\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.
5	Yet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.
6	This mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.
7	Unlike MaskLLM~\cite{fang2024maskllm}, which applies KL to static N:M weight masks, we address contextual activation sparsity where masks must generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.
8	
9	Gradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\partial \mathcal{L}_{\text{BCE}} / \partial s_j = \sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\S\ref{sec:method:gradient}).
10	KL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\% from magnitude-based masks (mean per-layer Jaccard distance).
11	A target-swap ablation shows KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information: BCE retrained on KL-derived targets approaches KL PPL ($\leq$0.65pp gap, 3-seed), while magnitude-trained BCE yields 28.13$\pm$0.20. Per-layer controls suggest coupling effects are task-dependent, with larger impacts on commonsense than knowledge tasks (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}).
12	
13	We instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.
14	At 30\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\ 10.30, both at 28.5\%) with downstream (MMLU 40.7$\pm$1.3\% vs.\ 65.3\% dense, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\times$).
15	At 50\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.
16	
17	Cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\%) with directional commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$19.9pp, $\sigma$=5.3pp; MMLU $-$2.8pp).
18	This paper is primarily an empirical study characterizing how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality across allocation strategies, sparsity levels, and model scales. The central finding---that output-distribution alignment consistently and substantially outperforms per-neuron classification for learned sparsity---is the paper's contribution. Without sparse kernel support, the predictor adds 6.4\% latency overhead; wall-clock speedup is not addressed (Appendix~\ref{app:latency}).
19	While individual components (KL divergence, Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation) are established techniques, their application to contextual activation sparsity prediction has not been explored, and the resulting empirical behaviors are non-obvious.
20	
21	Our contributions are:
22	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
23	\item \emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with per-layer decomposition showing the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\S\ref{sec:perlayer});
24	\item \emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence (7--14B) with task-type split: KL outperforms on PPL and commonsense but shows systematic knowledge regression, structural to output-distribution optimization (C4 control; \S\ref{sec:experiments});
25	\item \emph{Analytical:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\S\ref{sec:perlayer});
26	\item \emph{Analytical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\S\ref{sec:compensation}).
27	\end{itemize}
28	
29	\begin{figure}[t]
30	\centering
31	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}
32	\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}
33	\label{fig:overview}
34	\end{figure}
35	
```

```json
{
```

```json
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range",
    "newString": "the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nHow the training objective shapes learned sparsity prediction quality is an open question for SwiGLU LLMs~\\cite{shazeer2020glu}. SwiGLU FFN blocks exhibit high contextual sparsity, but their gated activation makes importance prediction challenging: neuron importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they predominantly rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nUnlike MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm}, which applies KL to static N:M weight masks, we address contextual activation sparsity where masks must generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (mean per-layer Jaccard distance).\nA target-swap ablation shows KL-discovered mask targets carry most of the quality information: BCE retrained on KL-derived targets approaches KL PPL ($\\leq$0.65pp gap, 3-seed), while magnitude-trained BCE yields 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Per-layer controls suggest coupling effects are task-dependent, with larger impacts on commonsense than knowledge tasks (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}).\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30, both at 28.5\\%) with downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\% vs.\\ 65.3\\% dense, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nCross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} (3-seed) confirms the PPL advantage ($-$17.3\\%) with directional commonsense gains but tied MMLU; preliminary evaluation on Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} shows consistent PPL improvement but reasoning decline (GSM8K $-$19.9pp, $\\sigma$=5.3pp; MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study characterizing how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality across allocation strategies, sparsity levels, and model scales. The central finding---that output-distribution alignment consistently and substantially outperforms per-neuron classification for learned sparsity---is the paper's contribution. Without sparse kernel support, the predictor adds 6.4\\% latency overhead; wall-clock speedup is not addressed (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\nWhile individual components (KL divergence, Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation) are established techniques, their application to contextual activation sparsity prediction has not been explored, and the resulting empirical behaviors are non-obvious.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with per-layer decomposition showing the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence (7--14B) with task-type split: KL outperforms on PPL and commonsense but shows systematic knowledge regression, structural to output-distribution optimization (C4 control; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments});\n\\item \\emph{Analytical:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});\n\\item \\emph{Analytical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Method overview: a lightweight per-layer predictor produces importance scores trained via KL divergence with Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation. At inference, scores feed TEAL's global top-$k$ allocation without compensation networks.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 20,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 20,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " ",
          " Our contributions are:",
          " \\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]",
          "-\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with per-layer decomposition showing the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) dwarfs the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});",
          "+\\item \\emph{Empirical:} Output-distribution alignment ranking across five losses, with per-layer decomposition showing the signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though confounded by train--inference mismatch (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});",
          " \\item \\emph{Empirical:} Cross-scale evidence (7--14B) with task-type split: KL outperforms on PPL and commonsense but shows systematic knowledge regression, structural to output-distribution optimization (C4 control; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments});",
          " \\item \\emph{Analytical:} Target-swap ablation suggests KL-discovered mask targets transfer to BCE; per-layer controls suggest task-dependent coupling effects (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer});",
          " \\item \\emph{Analytical:} KL-derived masks render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:compensation})."
        ]
      }
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Output-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction, outperforming BCE and MSE across five losses.
5	The KL predictor reduces PPL by 14.5\% over TEAL at 30\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B, with cross-architecture confirmation on Mistral-7B ($-$17.3\%) and Qwen-2.5-14B.
6	Gains are task-type dependent: KL improves commonsense reasoning but degrades knowledge-intensive tasks (C4 control confirms structural to KL), suggesting KL for commonsense/PPL and TEAL for knowledge tasks.
7	The signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch (\S\ref{sec:perlayer}); disentangling these factors remains an open question.
8	Downstream differences are directional ($n{=}3$, CIs overlap); larger-scale evaluation is needed. Code released upon acceptance.
9	
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOutput-distribution alignment is the primary design dimension for learned sparsity prediction, outperforming BCE and MSE across five losses.\nThe KL predictor reduces PPL by 14.5\\% over TEAL at 30\\% sparsity on LLaMA-3.1-8B, with cross-architecture confirmation on Mistral-7B ($-$17.3\\%) and Qwen-2.5-14B.\nGains are task-type dependent: KL improves commonsense reasoning but degrades knowledge-intensive tasks (C4 control confirms structural to KL), suggesting KL for commonsense/PPL and TEAL for knowledge tasks.\nThe signal type gap (9.47 PPL) exceeds the coupling breadth range (2.08 PPL), though the decomposition is confounded by train--inference mismatch (\\S\\ref{sec:perlayer}); disentangling these factors remains an open question.\nDownstream differences are directional ($n{=}3$, CIs overlap); larger-scale evaluation is needed.
```
